Byzantine-Robust and Differentially Private Federated Optimization under Weaker Assumptions
Rustem Islamov 1 , Grigory Malinovsky 2 , Alexander Gaponov 2 , Aurelien Lucchi 1 , Peter Richtárik 2 , Eduard Gorbunov 3

Abstract
Federated Learning (FL) enables heterogeneous clients to collaboratively train a shared model without centralizing their raw data, offering an inherent level of privacy. However, gradients and model updates can still leak sensitive information, while malicious servers may mount adversarial attacks such as Byzantine manipulation. These vulnerabilities highlight the need to address differential privacy (DP) and Byzantine robustness within a unified framework. Existing approaches, however, often rely on unrealistic assumptions such as bounded gradients, require auxiliary server-side datasets, or fail to provide convergence guarantees. We address these limitations by proposing Byz-Clip21-SGD2M , a new algorithm that integrates robust aggregation with double momentum and carefully designed clipping. We prove high-probability convergence guarantees under standard L L -smoothness and σ \sigma -sub-Gaussian gradient noise assumptions, thereby relaxing conditions that dominate prior work. Our analysis recovers state-of-the-art convergence rates in the absence of adversaries and improves utility guarantees under Byzantine and DP settings. Empirical evaluations on CNN and MLP models trained on MNIST further validate the effectiveness of our approach.

$\dagger$

1
Introduction

The rapid deployment of large-scale machine learning models has positioned Federated Learning (FL)
(
63
;
75
)
as a central paradigm, enabling a collection of potentially heterogeneous clients, ranging from smartphones and sensors to data centers, to collaboratively train a shared model without transmitting their raw data to a central server
(
67
;
111
)
. While this setting offers an inherent degree of privacy, it is far from complete: gradients and model parameters can still leak sensitive information
(
120
;
38
)
, and adversaries may exploit the distributed nature of FL to launch attacks such as membership inference, model stealing, or Byzantine manipulation
(
96
;
102
;
15
)
. This dual vulnerability highlights the need to study differential privacy, to mitigate privacy leakage, and Byzantine robustness, to ensure resilience against arbitrary or malicious participants, within a unified framework. Both challenges share the common difficulty of optimizing with corrupted or unreliable updates, whether the corruption arises from deliberate noise injection for privacy or from adversarial manipulations. Moreover, addressing these issues is not only of algorithmic importance: modern AI systems must also comply with evolving regulatory requirements, such as GDPR and the EU AI Act for privacy
(
88
;
89
)
, or the EU NIS2 Directive, Cyber Resilience Act, and NIST AI Risk Management Framework for security and robustness
(
26
;
90
;
101
)
.

In this context, we propose to study Byzantine robustness and differential privacy jointly, with the goal of establishing a principled foundation for robust, privacy-preserving federated learning and providing a pathway toward trustworthy AI in practice. A central open challenge is to move beyond the strong assumptions that dominate the current literature.
Most theoretical analyses in differential privacy are carried out under assumptions such as bounded gradients – an assumption that fails even for simple quadratic objectives
(
68
;
103
;
71
)
– or by focusing on the full-batch regime
(
98
)
.
Only recently have approaches emerged that achieve strong privacy guarantees while retaining favorable optimization performance under milder conditions
(
54
)
. Byzantine robustness has evolved largely as a separate line of work
(
72
)
, yet its analyses similarly depend on gradient boundedness to establish convergence guarantees
(
15
;
77
)
. Attempts to combine DP with Byzantine resilience remain even more limited: existing methods either assume access to an auxiliary server-side dataset to obtain a better gradient approximation
(
107
)
, rely on unrealistic aggregation rules
(
47
;
117
)
, impose restrictive bounded-gradient assumptions
(
9
)
.

Motivated by the shortcomings of prior theoretical results, we arrive at the following guiding question:

Is it possible to develop an algorithm that provably converges in the presence of malicious clients while simultaneously providing strong differential privacy guarantees under standard assumptions?

Main Contributions.

We provide an affirmative answer to the above question. In doing so, we make the following contributions:

•

We introduce
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
, a new algorithm that combines robust aggregation with double momentum and carefully designed clipping, achieving efficient performance under both Byzantine and differential privacy adversaries.

•

We provide a high-probability convergence analysis of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
under
L
-smoothness and
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian gradient noise, thereby relaxing assumptions that have been used in prior work (see Table
1
for details). Our results improve utility guarantees by avoiding unrealistic assumptions, while recovering state-of-the-art convergence rates in the absence of Byzantine or DP adversaries.

•

We complement our theoretical analysis with empirical validation, demonstrating the effectiveness of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
on CNN and MLP models trained on the MNIST dataset.

2
Related Works

Error Feedback.

Biased compression in federated learning reduces communication by transmitting compressed local updates that are not unbiased estimates of the original signal
(
2
;
14
;
25
)
. Common examples include Top-
K
sparsification
(
5
;
105
)
and sign-based compression
(
13
;
99
)
. While these methods are efficient in practice, the introduced bias can accumulate and affect convergence, requiring Error Feedback (
EF
)
(
95
)
techniques to mitigate its impact. Earlier works primarily focused on the single-node setting or relied on restrictive assumptions, such as bounded gradients, bounded compression error, or gradient dissimilarity, to establish convergence
(
99
;
100
;
62
)
. Moreover, the convergence rates of
EF
degrade in the presence of client heterogeneity, and this dependence is intrinsic rather than a proof artifact
(
44
)
.
To overcome these limitations,
(
91
)
proposed
EF21
, a variant whose guarantees do not depend on heterogeneity bounds. Nevertheless,
EF21
-SGD still requires increasingly large batch sizes to achieve a target accuracy
(
33
)
. Importantly, this limitation is not fundamental: recent work demonstrates that incorporating Heavy-Ball momentum removes the need for large batches
(
33
)
. Later,
EF21
was extended to several practical setups
(
32
;
73
)
. In a parallel line of research,
35
;
36
introduced
EControl
-type Error Feedback methods with convergence guarantees extending to the convex setting. Error Feedback has also been studied in decentralized
(
112
;
51
;
53
)
setting, for non-smooth
(
52
)
and composite
(
37
)
optimization problems, as well as in the context of second-order methods
(
94
;
84
;
55
)
.

Byzantine Robust Optimization.

Naively averaging updates from clients in a distributed setting lacks robustness since even a single Byzantine client can destabilize training
(
21
)
. To address this, numerous alternatives to averaging have been proposed to enhance robustness against adversarial updates
(
83
;
113
;
24
;
57
;
8
)
.

Nevertheless, permutation-invariant algorithms, i.e., the ones whose output is unchanged under shuffling of the stochastic gradients computed on different workers
(
57
, Definition B)
, alone cannot ensure convergence in the Byzantine regime
(
57
, Theorem II)
. This limitation can be alleviated by incorporating update shuffling and averaging in random groups
(
56
)
, variance-reduction techniques
(
106
;
43
)
, or preprocessing based on the averaging with nearest neighbors
(
7
)
. Beyond these, other approaches include concentration-based gradient filtering
(
4
;
6
)
, server-free protocols with random checks
(
41
)
, redundant computations
(
20
;
85
)
, and reputation-based mechanisms
(
92
;
87
;
110
)
. More recent works further extend these ideas to partial participation
(
74
)
, decentralized training
(
48
)
, and communication compression
(
86
)
. In most of these cases, the theoretical analysis is carried out under the
L
-smoothness and
\zeta^{2}
-gradient dissimilarity
1
1

1

More precisely, the mentioned works rely either on
\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)\|^{2}\leq\zeta^{2}
or
\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)\|^{2}\leq A_{\zeta}\|\nabla f(x)\|^{2}+\zeta^{2}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
assumptions, which have become standard in Byzantine robustness literature.

Differentially Private Optimization.

Differential privacy (DP) is typically achieved by clipping each client’s update and adding Gaussian noise, limiting the influence of any individual client
76
. In central DP, a trusted server adds noise before updating the global model, while in local DP, clients perturb updates before sending them, protecting privacy even from the server
(
58
;
10
)
. Local DP offers stronger privacy but reduces model utility, though this can be mitigated using secure shufflers or aggregators
(
29
;
16
)
. DP can also enable update compression without additional cost
(
19
;
49
)
. Private optimization methods, such as
DP-SGD

(
1
)
, enforce DP by clipping gradients and adding noise scaled to the clipping sensitivity. However, most convergence analyses neglect the bias introduced by clipping. For smooth functions, guarantees typically assume either
bounded gradient norms

(
67
;
116
;
78
;
103
;
71
;
104
)
or that
clipping is effectively inactive

(
80
;
115
)
. Recently, several works have provided improved analyses of
DP-Clip-SGD
and its variants. In the single-node setting, generalized smoothness has also been considered without restrictive requirements for
DP-Clip-SGD

(
61
)
. Next,
59
also focus on the single-node setting and provide high-probability results under the heavy-tailed noise assumption and arbitrary clipping level for
DP-Clip-SGD
.
118
consider a projected
SGD
-type method utilizing DP mean estimation and provide its in-expectation convergence bounds.
23
study SDE approximation of
DP-Clip-SGD
in the single-node setup. In the FL setting, analysis under realistic conditions in the presence of differential privacy has been studied in
54
;
97
;
98
.

Differentially Private and Byzantine Robust Methods.

Although substantial progress has been made separately in differential privacy and Byzantine robustness, their interaction in distributed learning is still not well understood. Recent efforts to address both aspects at once remain limited. A key limitation of existing theoretical work is the reliance on restrictive assumptions, often unrealistic and mainly introduced to simplify the analysis. For example, some studies focus on a single aggregation rule
(
47
)
, while others assume a particular noise structure near the minimizer or even access to a non-private external server-side dataset
(
107
)
. Other analyses restrict attention to the strongly convex case
(
34
;
9
)
or impose functional similarity assumptions that do not hold in heterogeneous federated learning
(
65
)
. Further limitations include assuming i.i.d. momentum buffers
(
45
)
or bounded-gradient conditions
(
46
;
34
;
9
)
. Taken together, these limitations highlight the significant challenges in jointly addressing differential privacy and Byzantine robustness.

We summarize the existing works and the assumptions underlying their convergence analyses for establishing utility bounds in
Table

1
. Compared to prior work, our theoretical analysis relies on the weakest set of assumptions.

3
Preliminaries

Problem Formulation.
In distributed training with potentially Byzantine clients, the goal is to minimize the empirical loss

\min_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\left[f(x)\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}f_{i}(x)\right],

(1)

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the model parameters. The set of regular clients is
\mathcal{G}
, with cardinality
G\coloneqq|\mathcal{G}|
, while the total number of clients is
n
. The remaining clients,
\mathcal{B}\coloneqq[n]\setminus\mathcal{G}
, are Byzantine: instead of following the prescribed learning algorithm, they may transmit arbitrary (possibly adversarial) vectors, and we assume they also observe the updates shared by the other clients
2
2

2

This assumption is admittedly strong and often unrealistic in practice. Nevertheless, it is widely adopted in the Byzantine-robust learning literature, since methods that guarantee robustness against such powerful adversaries also remain robust in more practical settings, where attackers are typically weaker.
. The number of Byzantine clients is
|\mathcal{B}|=\delta_{\rm byz}n
, with
\delta_{\rm byz}<\frac{1}{2}
to ensure that Byzantine clients do not form a majority; otherwise, problem (
1
) becomes unsolvable
(
81
)
. Each client
i\in[n]
is associated with a local loss function
f_{i}
, defined over its own data.

Differential Privacy.

Next, we will use the following classical definition of
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-Differential Privacy (
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP), which introduces
plausible deniability into the output of a learning algorithm.

Definition 3.1

(
(
28
)
)
.

A randomized method
\mathcal{M}\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-Differential Privacy (
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP for shortness) if for any two datasets
D,D^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
that differ in 1 sample and for any
S\subseteq\mathbb{R}

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{M}(D)\in S)\leq e^{\varepsilon}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{M}(D^{\prime})\in\mathcal{S})+\delta.

(2)

Decreasing
\varepsilon
and
\delta
strengthens privacy by making it harder to identify the particular data point that differs between neighboring datasets.

Robust Aggregation.

We follow the definition of
7
that covers many practical aggregation rules introduced in prior work
(
31
;
56
)
, including coordinate-wise median
(
113
)
and geometric median
(
83
)
.

Definition 3.2

(
(
7
)
)
.

Let the ratio of Byzantine clients be
\delta_{\rm byz}\coloneqq\nicefrac{{|\mathcal{B}|}}{{n}}<\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
and
c\geq 0
be a constant. The aggregation rule
RAgg
is said to be
(\delta_{\rm byz},c)
-robust if for any vectors
\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any subset
S\subset[n]
of size
n-|\mathcal{B}|
the output
\hat{x}={\rm RAgg}(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
satisfies

\|\hat{x}-\overline{x}\|^{2}\leq\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{n-|\mathcal{B}|}\sum_{i\in S}\|x_{i}-\overline{x}\|^{2}

(3)

where
\overline{x}=\frac{1}{n-|\mathcal{B}|}\sum_{i\in S}x_{i}.

Under this definition, robust aggregators deliver a worst-case guarantee: for
S=\mathcal{G}
, the output
\hat{x}
stays close to the mean
\overline{x}
of regular clients in squared distance. The rule can be further strengthened by input mixing steps such as
NNM

(
7
)
. Importantly, we require the worst-case property, i.e., (
3
) holds for every vector collection
\{x_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}
to enable our high-probability analysis. In contrast, prior work often studies stochastic aggregators under in-expectation guarantees
(
57
;
56
;
43
;
74
)
.

Assumptions.

In the convergence analysis of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
, we make use of two standard assumptions. The first describes the class of smooth functions, a standard assumption made in the non-convex optimization literature
(
40
;
17
)
.

Assumption 3.1
.

For all
i\in\mathcal{G}
,
f_{i}
is
L
-smooth, i.e., for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f_{i}(y)\|\leq L\|x-y\|.

(4)

Additionally, we have
f^{\star}\coloneqq\inf_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(x)>-\infty.

To simplify the presentation, we consider the worst smoothness constant across clients. Next, we introduce the assumption that formalizes the stochasticity in local gradients.

Assumption 3.2
.

Each client
i\in\mathcal{G}
has access to a
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian unbiased estimator
\nabla f_{i}(x,\xi)
of a local gradient
\nabla f_{i}(x)
, i.e., there exists a constant
\sigma\geq 0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\nabla f_{i}(x,\xi)\right]=\nabla f_{i}(x),\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\exp(\nicefrac{{\|\theta_{i}\|^{2}}}{{\sigma^{2}}})\right]\leq e,

(5)

where
\xi
denotes the source of stochasticity and
\theta_{i}\coloneqq\nabla f_{i}(x,\xi)-\nabla f_{i}(x).

Sub-Gaussian noise assumption is standard when analyzing high-probability complexity of
SGD
-type algorithms
(
39
;
79
;
70
)
.

Bounds on data heterogeneity are essential in the analysis of Byzantine-robust algorithms; without such assumptions, robustness is unachievable in fully arbitrary heterogeneous environments. A common approach is to assume a bounded average deviation of local gradients from the global gradient (gradient dissimilarity)
(
106
;
43
;
9
)
, as formalized in
3.3
. For our high-probability analysis, we instead require the maximum in
3.3a
. However, we impose this condition only at initialization
x^{0}
, whereas prior work typically assumes it holds uniformly over the entire space.

Assumption 3.3
.

The local gradients
\{\nabla f_{i}(x)\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}}
satisfy bounded
\zeta^{2}
-gradient dissimilarity for some
\zeta\geq 0
, i.e., for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have

\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x)-\nabla f(x)\|^{2}\leq\zeta^{2}.

(6)

Assumption 3.3a
.

The local gradients
\{\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}}
satisfy bounded
\zeta^{2}
-gradient dissimilarity for some
\zeta\geq 0
, i.e., we have

\max_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})-\nabla f(x^{0})\|^{2}\leq\zeta^{2}.

(7)

Remark 3.1
.

Our results also extend to a more general condition in which the right-hand side of (
7
) scales linearly with
\|\nabla f(x^{0})\|^{2}
, namely,
\max_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})-\nabla f(x^{0})\|^{2}\leq A_{\zeta}\|\nabla f(x^{0})\|^{2}+\zeta^{2}.
For clarity of presentation, the general result is provided in the supplementary materials, while we assume
A_{\zeta}=0
in the main text. Note that this assumption always holds, i.e., for any problem of the form (
1
), there exists
\zeta\geq 0
such that inequality (
7
) is satisfied. However, as we explicitly show in our theoretical results, the value of
\zeta
directly affects the convergence bounds since it is required to establish a bound
\Delta
on the initial value of the Lyapunov function
\Phi^{0}
introduced in (
9
).

4
Algorithm Design

We introduce the
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
algorithm, designed to operate under both Byzantine failures and differential privacy noise. The main steps are outlined in
Algorithm

1
, followed by a detailed discussion highlighting the key design innovations and their rationale.

Momentum Mechanism.

Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
employs two momentum buffers,
\{v_{i}^{t}\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}}
on the client side and
\{m_{i}^{t}\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}}
on the server side, both essential for mitigating noise during training. On the client side, momentum with parameter
\beta
smooths mini-batch gradient noise; without it, the method may fail to converge, as seen in algorithms such as
EF21-SGD
and
Clip21-SGD
, which provably fail to converge to arbitrary accuracy
\varepsilon
under mini-batch noise
(
33
;
54
)
. On the server side, momentum with parameter
\hat{\beta}
counteracts the accumulation of DP-noise in the buffers
\{m_{i}^{t}\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}}
, preventing it from destroying convergence due to fast accumulation of the DP noise in the updates.

Clipping.

It is crucial to bound model updates before injecting DP-noise. Without clipping, updates may have arbitrarily large norms, which would dominate the injected noise and invalidate the privacy guarantee. Clipping restricts each update to a controlled range, ensuring that the privacy guarantees remain meaningful and that the added noise achieves its intended effect. In
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
, we employ the standard norm-clipping mechanism, which rescales updates
relative to a threshold
\tau
:

\clip_{\tau}(x)=\begin{cases}\frac{\tau}{\|x\|}x,&\text{ if }\|x\|>\tau,\\
x,&\text{ if }\|x\|\leq\tau.\end{cases}

(8)

This procedure ensures a fixed sensitivity at each update, after which Gaussian noise is added to the clipped values to provide local
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP guarantees. Equivalently, the same effect can be interpreted as adding noise directly to the averaged client update. Consequently,
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
is compatible with all of the techniques discussed above and can also be deployed in the central DP setting, where
\sigma_{\omega}^{2}

scales down with the number of clients.

Error Feedback.

To mitigate client drift, which arises from the potentially different local data distributions across clients, we augment our method with an
EF21
-style error feedback mechanism on the client side. Error feedback controls the discrepancy between the true global update and local updates sent to the server. Incorporating this mechanism into
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
allows the algorithm to compensate for the bias introduced by heterogeneous client data, thereby ensuring convergence under larger levels of data heterogeneity. This design choice makes the method robust to one of the central difficulties in federated optimization and allows eliminating the need for the bounded gradient assumption imposed in prior work.

Robust Aggregation.

At the heart of robust
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
lies the aggregation rule
{\rm RAgg}
. To mitigate adversarial clients that attempt to disrupt training, we employ a robust aggregation rule satisfying
Definition

3.2
. Robust aggregation limits the damage that Byzantine clients can cause during training.

Challenges of Designing Differential Private and Byzantine-Robust Algorithms.

Designing algorithms that are both differentially private and Byzantine-robust is inherently challenging as the two objectives often conflict with each other. Differential privacy requires adding extra noise to the raw updates, which reduces their consistency and makes it easier for adversarial updates to remain undetected. Moreover, using robust aggregation to combine the DP-noisy updates can amplify the detrimental effects of noise, further hindering convergence. Consequently, theoretical analyses must be carried out with particular care and often under restrictive assumptions. Overcoming these limitations requires carefully designing algorithms that balance privacy, robustness, and practical learnability.

5
Theoretical Analysis

Algorithm 1

Byz-Clip21-SGD2M

0:

x^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
momentum parameters
\beta,\hat{\beta}\in(0,1],
step-size
\gamma>0
,
g_{i}^{0}=m_{i}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},
clipping parameter
\tau>0
, DP-noise variance
\sigma_{\omega}^{2}\geq 0

1:

for

t=0,\ldots,T-1

do

2:

x^{t+1}=x^{t}-\gamma g^{t}

3:

for

i\in\mathcal{G}

do

4:

v_{i}^{t+1}=(1-\beta)v_{i}^{t}+\beta\nabla f_{i}(x^{t+1},\xi^{t+1}_{i})

5:

\omega_{i}^{t+1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{\omega}^{2}\mathbf{I})

6:

c_{i}^{t+1}=\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t})+\omega_{i}^{t+1}

7:

g_{i}^{t+1}=g_{i}^{t}+\hat{\beta}\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t})

8:

end

for

9:

for

i\in\mathcal{B}

do

10:

c_{i}^{t+1}=(*)

sends arbitrary vector

11:

end

for

12:

m_{i}^{t+1}=m_{i}^{t}+\hat{\beta}c_{i}^{t+1}\penalty\

(∗)

13:

g^{t+1}={\rm RAgg}(m_{1}^{t+1},\dots,m_{n}^{t+1})

14:

end

for

(∗)
This step can be moved to the client side without affecting the DP guarantee and convergence analysis, thanks to the post-processing property
(
28
, Proposition 2.1)
.

Our convergence analysis is based on a carefully designed Lyapunov function

\Phi^{t}\coloneqq f(x^{t})-f^{\star}+\frac{8\gamma\beta}{\hat{\beta}^{2}\eta^{2}}\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|^{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{\hat{\beta}\eta}\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|g_{i}^{t}-v_{i}^{t}\|^{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{\beta}\|\overline{v}^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2},

(9)

where
\overline{v}^{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}v_{i}^{t}
, and
\eta\sim\tau
, whose value will be specified in the main convergence theorem.

The decrease of this Lyapunov function guarantees convergence of the algorithm, as it implies that
f(x^{t})-f^{\star}
decreases during the training. Moreover, as the iterations proceed, the auxiliary variables
v_{i}^{t}
and
g_{i}^{t}
become increasingly accurate approximations of
\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})
and
v_{i}^{t}
, respectively. In other words, the momentum buffers provide a smoothed estimate of the true gradients, demonstrating the benefit of momentum in reducing variance and effectively mitigating the noise introduced during training. The coefficients in the Lyapunov function are carefully chosen to balance its different components, ensuring that each term contributes at the same order and none dominates the others. The detailed proofs are deferred to
Appendices

D
and
E
.

Theorem 5.1

(Simplified)
.

Let Assumptions
3.1
,
3.2
, and
3.3a
hold, and
\alpha\in(0,1)
be a failure probability.
Let
\widetilde{B}_{\rm init}\coloneqq\max_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\{\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{0})\|\}>3\tau
,
\eta\sim\nicefrac{{\tau}}{{\widetilde{B}_{\rm init}}}
, and
\Delta\geq\Phi^{0}
for
\Phi^{0}
defined in (
9
). Then there exists a choice of hyperparameters
\gamma,\beta
, and
\hat{\beta}
(see a full statement in
Theorem

D.1
) such that the iterates of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
(Alg.
1
) run with DP-noise variance
\sigma_{\omega}^{2}>0
and
(c,\delta_{\rm byz})
-robust aggregator
(\delta_{\rm byz}>0)
satisfy with probability at least
1-\alpha
that

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Delta_{0}(R+R^{2/3})\right),

where

R=\frac{\sqrt{d}\sigma_{\omega}}{\sqrt{GT}\tau}+\frac{\sqrt{c\delta_{\rm byz}d}\sigma_{\omega}}{\sqrt{T}\tau}+\sqrt{c\delta_{\rm byz}},\quad F^{0}\coloneqq f(x^{0})-f^{\star},

\Delta_{0}\coloneqq\sqrt{L\Delta}(\sqrt{L\Delta}+\widetilde{B}+\sigma)=\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(LF^{0}+\zeta^{2}+\sigma(\sqrt{LF^{0}}+\zeta)),

and
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}
hides constant and logarithmic factors and higher order terms decreasing in
T
.
3
3

3

The failure probability
\alpha
appears in the logarithmic terms similar to prior work
(
30
;
12
;
93
)
.

Remark 5.1
.

We stress that
3.3a
is invoked only to bound the constant
\Delta_{0}
; the convergence guarantee itself still holds without this condition due to the use of clipping. This general result highlights that bounded heterogeneity is primarily used to control the norms of the updates. In our setting, the updates
c_{i}^{t+1}
are used to update the momenta
m_{i}^{t+1}
, which are then aggregated through
{\rm RAgg}
. The stability of
m_{i}^{t+1}
is maintained by the clipping operator, since without such control the norm could otherwise grow unbounded. Moreover, due to our design choice
\hat{\beta}\sim 1/T
, the growth rate of the norm is limited to
\mathcal{O}(t\tau\hat{\beta})=\mathcal{O}(1)
, ensuring that the momenta remain well-behaved throughout the training process.

Figure 1
:
Performance of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
,
Byz-Clip-SGD
(
Algorithm

3
), and
Safe-DSHB
(
Algorithm

4
) when training CNN (top line) and MLP (bottom line) models on the MNIST dataset for different numbers of Byzantine clients and privacy budgets, when Byzantine clients use
IPM
attack.

Next, we consider the implication of this theorem when
\sigma_{\omega}
is set in a special way such that each step of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
satisfies local-
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP.

Corollary 5.1

(Simplified)
.

Under the setup of
Theorem

5.1
, let
\sigma_{\omega}=\Theta\left(\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon}\sqrt{T\log(\frac{T}{\delta})\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}\right)
for some
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
. Then there exists a choice of hyperparameters
\gamma,\beta
, and
\hat{\beta}
(see a full statement in
Corollary

D.1
) such that all
T
iterations of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
satisfy local
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-DP with robustness-privacy-utility trade-off bounded as

\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}\leq\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\Delta_{0}(R+R^{2/3})\right),

with probability at least
1-\alpha
, where

R={\color[rgb]{1,0.5,0}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{G}\varepsilon}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\sqrt{c\delta_{\rm byz}}\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\varepsilon}}+{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\sqrt{c\delta_{\rm byz}}}.

In
Corollary

5.1
, the
first term
corresponds to the error arising from privacy, while the
last term
reflects the error introduced by the presence of Byzantine clients. The
middle term
represents the additional penalty incurred when enforcing both requirements simultaneously. Our utility bound takes a similar form as the lower bound in the strongly convex case
(
9
)
, which demonstrates that our analysis is essentially tight. In particular,
Theorem

5.1
shows that the impacts of privacy and Byzantine adversaries multiply. The privacy analysis of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
then follows from the well-known result in (
28
in combination with the advanced composition theorem (
(
28
, Theorem 3.20)
), we obtain that all
T
iterations of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
are local
(\varepsilon,\delta)
-differentially private.

Comparison to prior work.

We analyze
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
under the standard
L
-smoothness and
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian assumptions, thereby improving over earlier work that required the far more restrictive bounded-gradient condition
(
119
;
9
)
. To our knowledge, the only high-probability study of optimization algorithms in the simultaneous presence of DP and Byzantine adversaries is due to
107
; however, their analysis is limited to a single attack model and relies on strong assumptions, thereby limiting its applicability. Other works rely on expectation-based analysis, yet still impose restrictive assumptions; see
Table

1
for a concrete comparison. By contrast, our convergence guarantee for
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
depends only logarithmically on the failure probability
\alpha
, which is consistent with standard high-probability analyses, and holds for any aggregation rule satisfying
Definition

3.2
.

Main Challenges in the Analysis.

Server-side aggregation induces a bias between the server aggregate
g^{t}
and the honest-average update
\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}m_{i}^{t}
. This bias scales multiplicatively with the DP-noise variance
\sigma_{\omega}
and
c\delta_{\rm byz}
; controlling it without assuming that the gradients are bounded requires a careful analysis with well-calibrated hyperparameters. To address this, we treat clipping as a contractive compressor, with the complication that its contraction factor is input-dependent, which motivates a high-probability analysis. Using a refined inductive argument, we show that the inputs to the clipping operator remain bounded with high probability. Finally, obtaining only poly-logarithmic dependence on the failure probability
\alpha
hinges on a careful decomposition of the noise terms, each controlled via concentration inequalities.

5.1
Convergence in Special Cases

We now examine several special cases of our bound, which showcase both the tightness of our analysis and the broad applicability of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
. Most follow directly from
Theorem

5.1
.

Absence of DP and Byzantine Adversaries.

Setting
\sigma_{\omega}=0
and
\delta_{\rm byz}=0
significantly simplifies the choice of hyperparameters. In particular, we can set
\gamma\sim\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{L}}
and
\hat{\beta}=1
. The following corollary provides a formal convergence in this setting, obtained directly from
Theorem

5.1
.

Figure 2
:
Performance of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
,
Byz-Clip-SGD
(
Algorithm

3
), and
Safe-DSHB
(
Algorithm

4
) when training CNN (top line) and MLP (bottom line) models on the MNIST dataset for different numbers of Byzantine clients and privacy budgets, when Byzantine clients use label flipping attack.

Corollary 5.2

(Simplified)
.

Under assumptions of
Theorem

5.1
, let
\sigma_{\omega}=0
and
\delta_{\rm byz}=0
. Then, there exists a set of hyperparameters
\gamma\sim\nicefrac{{\beta}}{{L}}
and
\hat{\beta}=1
such that the iterates of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
satisfy with probability at least
1-\alpha
that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}
is bounded by

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{L\Delta(1+\widetilde{B}/\tau)}{T}+\frac{\sigma(\sqrt{L\Delta}+\widetilde{B}+\sigma)}{\sqrt{GT}}\right)

This result yields the standard
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{GT}}})
convergence rate, which matches known lower bounds in this regime
(
11
)
. Importantly, it is established under only the standard assumptions of
L
-smoothness and
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian gradient noise, thereby improving over prior results
(
69
;
80
;
10
)
.

DP in the absence of Byzantine Clients.

As a next special case, we consider the setting where data is protected solely through DP noise injection, with no Byzantine clients present (i.e.,
\delta_{\rm byz}=0
). In this regime, the privacy-utility trade-off can be obtained by simply setting
\delta_{\rm byz}=0
in
Corollary

5.1
.

Corollary 5.3

(Simplified)
.

Under the setup of
Theorem

5.1
, let
\sigma_{\omega}=\Theta\left(\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon}\sqrt{T\log(\frac{T}{\delta})\log(\frac{1}{\delta})}\right)
for some
\varepsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
and
\delta_{\rm byz}=0
. Then there exists a choice of hyperparameters
\gamma,\beta
, and
\hat{\beta}
such that the iterates of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
satisfy with probability at least
1-\alpha
that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}
is bounded by

\sqrt{L\Delta}(\sqrt{L\Delta}+\widetilde{B}+\sigma)\cdot\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{G}\varepsilon}+\left(\frac{\sqrt{d}}{\sqrt{G}\varepsilon}\right)^{2/3}\right).

In this setting, our general theorem subsumes the results of
54
. Moreover, in modern applications where the model size
d
is much larger than the number of regular clients
G

(
18
;
22
)
, the dominant privacy–utility term is
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\nicefrac{{\sqrt{d}}}{{\sqrt{G}\varepsilon}})
, matching known lower bounds in this regime
(
27
)
. Moreover, unlike prior work, this result does not rely on unrealistic problem assumptions.

Byzantine robustness in the absence of DP-noise.

In this regime, clipping, originally required for privacy guarantees, is no longer needed. Accordingly, we set
\tau=+\infty
and
\hat{\beta}=1
in
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
, which reduces to the algorithm analyzed by
56
; see
Algorithm

2
. This simplification also removes the need to store the server-side momentum buffers
\{m_{i}^{t}\}_{i=1}^{n}
. Since applying
Theorem

5.1
directly in this case would yield weaker guarantees, we instead develop a dedicated high-probability analysis, leading to much simpler step-size conditions. Furthermore, the Lyapunov function itself becomes considerably simpler, as the buffers
\{g_{i}^{t}\}_{i\in\mathcal{G}}
are no longer required (see
Equation

95
).

Theorem 5.2

(Simplified)
.

Let
3.1
,
3.2
and
3.3
hold. Then there exists a choice of hyperparameters
\gamma,\beta
, and
\hat{\beta}=1
such that the iterates of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
with
\sigma_{\omega}=0
satisfy with probability at least
1-\alpha
that
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=0}^{T-1}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}
is bounded by

\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}\left(\frac{LF^{0}}{T}+\frac{\sigma(\sqrt{LF^{0}}+\sigma/\sqrt{G}+\sqrt{c\delta_{\rm byz}}(\zeta+\sigma)}{\sqrt{GT}}+c\delta_{\rm byz}\zeta^{2}\right).

We establish that
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
converges at rate
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\sqrt{GT}}})
to a neighborhood of size
\widetilde{\mathcal{O}}(c\delta_{\rm byz}\zeta^{2})
, matching the in-expectation lower bound of
56
.

6
Experiments

To demonstrate the efficacy of the proposed algorithm, we test the performance of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
against
Byz-Clip-SGD
(
Algorithm

3
) and
Safe-DSHB
(
Algorithm

4
)
(
9
)
when training CNN and MLP models on the MNIST dataset
(
66
)
. We fix the number of regular clients to
20
and distribute the dataset among them equally. Then, we add Byzantine clients that perform
IPM
attack
(
108
)
on the vectors transmitted from the clients to the server. In particular, each Byzantine client computes the average of the transmitted vectors of regular clients and multiplies the average by
-10
as described in
109
(
Figure

1
). We test performance when varying the number of Byzantine clients and the privacy budget
\varepsilon\in\{3,8,13,18,23\}
. For each algorithm we perform an extensive tuning of the learning rate parameter in
\{10,1,10^{-1},10^{-2},10^{-3}\}
and clipping threshold
\tau\in\{1,10^{-1},10^{-2},10^{-4},10^{-5},10^{-6}\}
. For
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
and
Safe-DSHB
, we fix the local momentum parameter
\beta=0.1
, while we use
\hat{\beta}=0.01
for
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
. In such a setting, every algorithm allocates an equal amount of privacy budget for tuning, since the set of tested hyperparameters remains the same across algorithms. We test the performance of algorithms with a batch size
64
for MLP and
32
for CNN training. Consistent with our theory, where we assume access to
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian stochastic gradients, we disable privacy amplification by sub-sampling and inject DP noise with standard deviation
\sigma_{\omega}=\frac{\tau}{\varepsilon}\sqrt{T\log\left(\nicefrac{{1}}{{\delta}}\right)},
where
T
is the total number of iterations.

The results in
Figure

1
present the mean and one standard deviation across three random seeds. In all configurations,
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
is competitive, matching or surpassing the baselines in test accuracy. These findings align with our theory and provide empirical evidence that
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
is an effective method for training under DP noise injection and Byzantine attacks. Additional experimental results and training details are summarized in
Appendix

F
.

7
Conclusion and Limitations

In this work, we present
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
, a new algorithm that admits provable convergence under standard
L
-smoothness and
\sigma
-sub-Gaussian gradient noise in the simultaneous presence of DP and Byzantine adversaries. Several directions merit further study:
(i)
establishing lower bounds without the bounded-gradient assumption of
9
;
(ii)
tightening our convergence guarantees to match known lower bounds exactly when DP and Byzantine adversaries are considered separately;
(iii)
extending the analysis of
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M
to heavy-tailed gradient noise and relaxed smoothness assumptions
(
114
;
3
)
;
(iv)
strengthening the privacy guarantees via amplification by data and client subsampling.

8

In our analysis, we make use of the following quantities

\displaystyle\overline{v}^{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}v_{i}^{t},\quad\overline{g}^{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}g_{i}^{t},\quad\overline{m}^{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}m_{i}^{t},\quad\Omega_{i}^{t}\coloneqq\sum_{l=1}^{t}\omega_{i}^{l},\quad\overline{\Omega}^{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\Omega_{i}^{t},

\displaystyle\theta^{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\theta_{i}^{t}\nabla f(x^{t},\xi^{t})\coloneqq\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i}).

(10)

Next, we derive the relation between
\overline{m}^{t}
and
\overline{g}^{t}
of the form

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{t}=\overline{g}^{t}+\hat{\beta}\Omega^{t}

(11)

Indeed, we have
\overline{m}^{0}=\overline{g}^{0}
by initialization of
Algorithm

1
. Assume that the relation holds at iteration
t
, let us show that it also holds at iteration
t+1:

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{t+1}=

\displaystyle\overline{m}^{t}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}[\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t})+\omega_{i}^{t+1}]

\displaystyle\overset{\text{Ind. Asmp.}}{=}\;

\displaystyle\overline{g}^{t}+\hat{\beta}\overline{\Omega}^{t}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t})+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\omega_{i}^{t+1}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{eq:def_averages}}{=}\;

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}[g_{i}^{t}+\hat{\beta}\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t})]+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}[\Omega_{i}^{t}+\omega_{i}^{t+1}]

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}g_{i}^{t+1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\Omega_{i}^{t+1}

\displaystyle=\;

\displaystyle\overline{g}^{t+1}+\hat{\beta}\overline{\Omega}^{t+1}.

(12)

Similarly, we obtain

\displaystyle m_{i}^{t}=g_{i}^{t}+\hat{\beta}\Omega_{i}^{t}.

(13)

Appendix B
Useful Lemmas

First, we start with a key property of the clipping operator.

Lemma 1

(Lemma 4.1 in
[
60
]
)
.

The clipping operator satisfies for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

\displaystyle\|\clip_{\tau}(x)-x\|\leq\max\{\|x\|-\tau,0\}.

(14)

Next, in our high probability analysis, we make use of the following concentration inequality.

Lemma 2

(Lemma C.3 in
[
42
]
)
.

Let
\{\xi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
be the sequence of random vectors with values in
\mathbb{R}^{n}
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[\xi_{k}\mid\xi_{k-1},\dots,\xi_{1}\right]=0\text{ almost surely, }\forall k\in\{1,\dots,N\},

and set
S_{N}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{N}\xi_{k}
. Assume that the sequence
\{\xi_{k}\}_{k=1}^{N}
are sub-Gaussian, i.e.

\mathbb{E}\left[\exp\left(\nicefrac{{\|\xi_{k}\|^{2}}}{{\sigma_{k}^{2}}}\mid\xi_{k-1},\dots,\xi_{1}\right)\right]\leq\exp(1)\text{ almost surely, }\forall k\in\{1,\dots,N\},

where
\sigma_{2},\dots,\sigma_{N}
are some positive numbers. Then for all
\gamma\geq 0

\mathbb{P}\left(\|S_{N}\|\geq(\sqrt{2}+2\gamma)\sqrt{\sum_{k=1}^{N}\sigma_{k}^{2}}\right)\leq\exp(-\nicefrac{{\gamma^{2}}}{{3}}).

(15)

Appendix C
Descent Lemmas

Lemma 3
.

Let
f
be
L
-smooth,
F^{t}\coloneqq f(x^{t})-f^{\star},

\{x^{t}\}
be generated by
Algorithm

1
with
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{2L}
. Then we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f(x^{t+1})&\leq f(x^{t})-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\frac{1}{4\gamma}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\nabla f(x^{t})-\overline{v}^{t}\|^{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t}\|^{2}\\
&\qquad+\;2\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\|\Omega^{t}\|^{2}+16\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}c\delta_{\rm byz}\tau^{2}T^{2}+16\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2},\end{aligned}

(16)

Proof.

Using the derivations from
[
54
]
, Lemma 2 we first get

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq f(x^{t})-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{4}\|g^{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})-g^{t}\|^{2}.

We continue the derivations as follows

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}f(x^{t})-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma}{4}\|g^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\nabla f(x^{t})-\overline{v}^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\overline{v}^{t}-\overline{g}^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\overline{g}^{t}-\overline{m}^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\;2\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t}-g^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}f(x^{t})-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\frac{1}{4\gamma}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\nabla f(x^{t})-\overline{v}^{t}\|^{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\;2\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\|\Omega^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t}-g^{t}\|^{2},

(17)

where
(i)
follows from Jensen’s inequality applied to
\|\cdot\|^{2}
,

(ii)
is obtained using again Jensen’s inequality applied to
\|\cdot\|^{2}
, the update rule of
x^{t},
and (
11
). Now we bound the term
\|\overline{m}^{t}-g^{t}\|^{2}
using the properties of the aggregator. We have

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}-g^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\|\overline{m}^{t}-{\rm RAgg}(m_{1}^{t},\dots,m_{n}^{t})\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\left\|\overline{m}^{t}-m_{i}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G^{2}}\sum_{j,l\in\mathcal{G}}\left\|m_{j}^{t}-m_{l}^{t}\right\|^{2}=\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G^{2}}\sum_{j,l\in\mathcal{G},j\neq l}\left\|m_{j}^{t}-m_{l}^{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G(G-1)}\sum_{j,l\in\mathcal{G},j\neq l}\|m_{j}^{t}-m_{l}^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{=}\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G(G-1)}\sum_{j,l\in\mathcal{G},j\neq l}\|g_{j}^{t}+\hat{\beta}\Omega_{j}^{t}-g_{l}^{t}-\hat{\beta}\Omega_{l}^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(vi)}{\leq}\frac{8c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\left\|\hat{\beta}\sum_{s=0}^{t}\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{s}-g_{i}^{s-1})\right\|^{2}+\frac{8c\delta_{\rm byz}\hat{\beta}^{2}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{(vii)}{\leq}8c\delta_{\rm byz}\hat{\beta}^{2}(t+1)^{2}\tau^{2}+\frac{8c\delta_{\rm byz}\hat{\beta}^{2}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2},

(18)

where
(iii)
follows from the definition of
{\rm RAgg}
;
(iv)
– from Young’s inequality,
(v)
– from (
13
),
(vi)
– from Young’s inequality and update rule of
g_{i}^{t}
together with the initialization
g_{i}^{0}=0
,
(vii)
– from Young’s inequality. Plugging (
18
) in (
C
) and noting that
(t+1)\leq T
, we get

\displaystyle f(x^{t+1})

\displaystyle\leq f(x^{t})-\frac{\gamma}{2}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|^{2}-\frac{1}{4\gamma}\|x^{t+1}-x^{t}\|^{2}+2\gamma\|\nabla f(x^{t})-\overline{v}^{t}\|^{2}+\frac{2\gamma}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad+\;2\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\|\Omega^{t}\|^{2}+16\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}c\delta_{\rm byz}\tau^{2}T^{2}+16\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2},

(19)

that finalizes the proof.
∎

Lemma 4
.

Let each
f_{i}
be
L
-smooth. Then, for the iterates of
Algorithm

1
we have the following inequality with probability
1

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|&\leq(1-\hat{\beta})\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}\max\left\{0,\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau\right\}+\beta L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t}\|+\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|\\
&\qquad+\;2\beta\hat{\beta}\tau TL\gamma\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+2\beta\hat{\beta}L\gamma\sqrt{\frac{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2}}+\beta\|\theta^{t+1}_{i}\|,\end{aligned}

where
\theta^{t}_{i}\coloneqq\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}).

Proof.

First, we follow the steps of the proof of
54
to derive

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\hat{\beta})\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}\max\left\{0,\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau\right\}+\beta L\gamma\|g^{t}\|+\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\beta\|\theta^{t+1}_{i}\|.

(20)

Next, we continue as follows

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\hat{\beta})\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}\max\left\{0,\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau\right\}+\beta L\gamma\|g^{t}-\overline{m}^{t}\|+\beta L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|+\beta\|\theta^{t+1}_{i}\|.

(21)

Now we perform similar derivations as in
Lemma

3
to bound
\|g^{t}-\overline{m}^{t}\|
(see (
18
)), which finally gives the bound

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq(1-\hat{\beta})\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}\max\left\{0,\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau\right\}+\beta L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t}\|+\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\beta L\gamma\sqrt{8c\delta_{\rm byz}\hat{\beta}^{2}T^{2}\tau^{2}+8\hat{\beta}^{2}\frac{c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2}}+\beta\|\theta^{t+1}_{i}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{\leq}(1-\hat{\beta})\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}\max\left\{0,\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau\right\}+\beta L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t}\|+\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;2\beta\hat{\beta}\tau TL\gamma\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+2\beta\hat{\beta}L\gamma\sqrt{\frac{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2}}+\beta\|\theta^{t+1}_{i}\|,

(22)

where
(i)
follows from the triangle inequality:
\sqrt{s+q}\leq\sqrt{s}+\sqrt{q}
for any
s,q\geq 0.

∎

Lemma 5
.

Let each
f_{i}
be
L
-smooth, and
\Delta\geq\Phi^{0}.
Assume that the following inequalities hold for the iterates generated by
Byz-Clip21-SGD2M

1.

\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}
,

2.

\beta\in[0,1]
,
\hat{\beta}\in[0,\nicefrac{{1}}{{24}}]
,

3.

\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T
,

4.

\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T;

5.

\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

6.

\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq B_{\rm init}
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

7.

\|\Omega^{t}\|\leq a;

8.

\|\Omega_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq\hat{a}
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

9.

\|\theta_{i}^{t}\|\leq b
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

10.

\Phi^{t-1}\leq 2\Delta.

Then we have

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}\|\leq\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T.

(23)

Proof.

We start as follows

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\left\|\overline{m}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}[\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})+\omega_{i}^{t}]\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\overline{m}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\left[\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})+(v_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))+\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})-(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\;\left.-\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}g_{i}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\omega_{i}^{t}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\overline{m}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\left[\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})+(v_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t}))+\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})-(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})\right]\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\;\left.-\overline{g}^{t-1}+(1-\hat{\beta})\overline{g}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\omega_{i}^{t}\right\|.

where
(i)
follows from the update rule of
\overline{m}^{t}
. We continue the derivations as follows

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\left\|\overline{m}^{t-1}-\overline{g}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\omega_{i}^{t}\right\|+\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})-(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\left\|\overline{g}^{t-1}+\hat{\beta}\Omega^{t-1}-\overline{g}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\omega_{i}^{t}\right\|+\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t-1})\|+\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t-1})-\nabla f(x^{t})\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})-(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})\|+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|(1-\beta)v_{i}^{t-1}+\beta\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|,

(24)

where
(ii)
follows from the triangle inequality,
(iii)
– from (
11
), triangle inequality, and update rule of
v_{i}^{t}
. Using the definition of
\Omega^{t}
we continue as follows

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\|\Omega^{t}\|+\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t-1})\|+\hat{\beta}L\gamma\|g^{t-1}\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\max\left\{0,\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau\right\}+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\left((1-\beta)\|v_{i}^{t-1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|+\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\|\Omega^{t}\|+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2L(f(x^{t-1})-f^{\star})}+(2-\beta)\hat{\beta}L\gamma\|g^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}\beta}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}(1-\beta)\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t-1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})\|

\displaystyle\overset{(vi)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\|\Omega^{t}\|+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2L\Phi^{t-1}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}(2-\beta)\hat{\beta}L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t-1}-g^{t-1}\|+{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}2}(2-\beta)\hat{\beta}L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}\beta}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}(1-\beta)\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t-1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})\|,

where
(iv)
follows from
L
-smoothness, update rule of
x^{t},
the clipping property from
Lemma

1
, and triangle inequality,
(v)
– from
L
-smoothness, triangle inequality, the update rule of
x^{t}
, the definition of
\theta^{t}_{i}
, and the assumption
5
of the lemma,
(vi)
– from the definition of
\Phi^{t}
and triangle inequality. Now we use (
18
) and the assumptions of the lemma to derive

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(vii)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{4L\Delta}+4\hat{\beta}L\gamma\sqrt{8c\delta_{\rm byz}\hat{\beta}^{2}T^{2}\tau^{2}+\hat{\beta}^{2}\frac{8c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t-1}\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\;4\hat{\beta}L\gamma\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)+\hat{\beta}\beta b

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\left(\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\overset{(viii)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{4L\Delta}+8\hat{\beta}^{2}T\tau L\gamma\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+8\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\hat{\beta}\beta b

\displaystyle\qquad+\;4\hat{\beta}L\gamma\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+3\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\left(\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right),

(25)

where
(vii)
follows from assumptions
3
-
5
,
7
,
9
,
10

(viii)
– from inequality
\sqrt{s+q}\leq\sqrt{s}+\sqrt{q}
for any
a,b\geq 0
. Rearranging terms, we derive

\displaystyle\|\overline{m}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{L\Delta}[2\hat{\beta}+32\hat{\beta}L\gamma+8(1-\hat{\beta})+2\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)]+(B_{\rm init}-\tau)[12\hat{\beta}L\gamma+3(1-\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;b[12\hat{\beta}L\gamma+3(1-\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}\beta+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)]+a[\hat{\beta}+12\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+\hat{\beta}^{2}(1-\beta)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}[8\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+12\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+4\hat{\beta}^{2}(1-\beta)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\tau T\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}[\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}^{2}(1-\beta)+3\hat{\beta}(1-\hat{\beta})+12\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+8\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma].

(26)

For the first coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\;2\hat{\beta}+32\hat{\beta}L\gamma+8(1-\hat{\beta})+2\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\leq 8

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;2\hat{\beta}+32\hat{\beta}L\gamma+2\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\leq 8\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;8L\gamma\leq 1,

(27)

where the last inequality is satisfied by the choice of the step-size
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}.

For the second coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\;12\hat{\beta}L\gamma+3(1-\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\leq 3

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;12\hat{\beta}L\gamma+\hat{\beta}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\leq 3\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;24L\gamma\leq 1,

(28)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}
. For the third coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\;12\hat{\beta}L\gamma+3(1-\hat{\beta})+\hat{\beta}\beta+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\leq 3

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;12\hat{\beta}L\gamma+\hat{\beta}\beta+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\leq 3\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;24L\gamma\leq 1,

(29)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}
. For the fourth coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}+12\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+\hat{\beta}^{2}(1-\beta)\leq 3\hat{\beta}\Leftarrow 12L\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}+\hat{\beta}^{2}\leq 2\hat{\beta}\Leftarrow 12L\gamma\leq 1,

(30)

where the second last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L},
and the last inequality holds by the choice of the momentum parameter
\hat{\beta}\leq 1.

For the fifth coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\;8\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+12\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+4\hat{\beta}^{2}(1-\beta)\leq\hat{\beta}\Leftarrow 20\hat{\beta}L\gamma+4\hat{\beta}\leq 1,

(31)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}
and momentum parameter
\hat{\beta}\leq\frac{1}{24}.

For the sixth coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\;\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}^{2}(1-\beta)+3\hat{\beta}(1-\hat{\beta})+12\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma+8\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma\leq 3

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\hat{\beta}^{2}+20\hat{\beta}^{2}L\gamma\leq 3\hat{\beta}

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;20\hat{\beta}L\gamma\leq\frac{3}{2}

\displaystyle\Leftarrow

\displaystyle\;\frac{40}{3}\hat{\beta}L\gamma\leq 1,

(32)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}
and momentum parameter
\hat{\beta}\leq 1
.
∎

Lemma 6
.

Let each
f_{i}
be

-smooth,

,

. Assume that the following inequalities hold for the iterates generated by

;

12L\gamma\leq\beta
;

\beta,\hat{\beta}\in[0,1];

\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T
,

5.

\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T;

6.

\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

7.

\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq B_{\rm init}
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

8.

\|\Omega^{t}\|\leq a;

9.

\|\Omega_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq\hat{a}
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

10.

\|\theta_{i}^{t}\|\leq b
for all
i\in\mathcal{G};

11.

\Phi^{t-1}\leq 2\Delta.

Then we have

\displaystyle\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|\leq\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T.

(33)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-(1-\beta)v_{i}^{t-1}-\beta\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i})\|

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|+\beta\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i})\|

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})\|+(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|+\beta\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}(1-\beta)L\gamma\|g^{t-1}\|+(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|+\beta\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{\leq}(1-\beta)L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t-1}-g^{t-1}\|+(1-\beta)L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|+(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|+\beta\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(vi)}{\leq}(1-\beta)L\gamma\left(2\hat{\beta}\tau T\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+2\hat{\beta}\sqrt{\frac{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t-1}\|^{2}}\right)+(1-\beta)L\gamma\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\beta\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|+(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(vii)}{\leq}2\hat{\beta}L\gamma\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\tau T+2\hat{\beta}L\gamma(1-\beta)\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\beta b

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\beta)L\gamma\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\beta)\left(\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{L\Delta}[8L\gamma+2(1-\beta)]+(B_{\rm init}-\tau)[3L\gamma+\nicefrac{{3(1-\beta)}}{{2}}]+b[3L\gamma+\beta+\nicefrac{{3(1-\beta)}}{{2}}]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\hat{\beta}a[3L\gamma+(1-\beta)]+\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\hat{a}[2L\gamma+L\gamma+4(1-\beta)]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T[2L\gamma+3L\gamma+\frac{3}{2}(1-\beta)],

where
(i)
follows from the update rule of
v_{i}^{t}
,
(ii)
-
(iii)
– from triangle inequality and definition of
\theta^{t}_{i}
,
(iv)
– from
L
-smoothness and the update rule of
x^{t}
,
(v)
– from triangle inequality

(vi)
– from (
18
) and inequality
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for any
a,b\geq 0
,
(vii)
– from the assumptions
4
-
7
,
9
,
10
of the lemma.

For the first coefficient, we have

\displaystyle 8L\gamma+2(1-\beta)\leq 2\Leftarrow 4L\gamma\leq\beta

(34)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
12L\gamma\leq\beta
. For the second coefficient, we have

\displaystyle 3L\gamma+\frac{3}{2}(1-\beta)\leq\frac{3}{2}\Leftarrow 2L\gamma\leq\beta,

(35)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
12L\gamma\leq\beta
. For the third coefficient, we have

\displaystyle 3L\gamma+\beta+\frac{3}{2}(1-\beta)\leq\frac{3}{2}\Leftarrow 6L\gamma\leq\beta,

(36)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
12L\gamma\leq\beta
. For the fourth coefficient, we have

\displaystyle 3L\gamma+(1-\beta)\leq 1\Leftarrow 3L\gamma\leq\beta,

(37)

the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
12L\gamma\leq\beta.
For the fifth coefficient, we have

\displaystyle\;2L\gamma+L\gamma+4(1-\beta)\leq 4\Leftarrow 3L\gamma\leq 4\beta,

(38)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
12L\gamma\leq\beta
. For the sixth coefficient, we have

\displaystyle 2L\gamma+3L\gamma+\frac{3}{2}(1-\beta)\leq\frac{3}{2}\Leftarrow\frac{10}{3}L\gamma\leq\beta,

(39)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
12L\gamma\leq\beta
. This concludes the proof.

Lemma 7
.

Let each
f_{i}
be
L
-smooth,
\Delta\geq\Phi^{0}
,
B_{\rm init}>\tau
. Assume that the following inequalities hold for the iterates generated by

\gamma\leq\frac{1}{24L}
;

2.

\beta\in[0,1];

3.

\hat{\beta}\leq\min\left\{\frac{\sqrt{L\Delta}}{a},\frac{\sqrt{L\Delta}}{4\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}},1\right\};

for all

for all

for all

\Phi^{t-1}\leq 2\Delta.

Then we have

\displaystyle\|\overline{g}^{t}\|\leq\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T.

(40)

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\|\overline{g}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(i)}{=}\left\|\overline{g}^{t-1}+\frac{1}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\hat{\beta}\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|\hat{\beta}\nabla f(x^{t})+\hat{\beta}(\overline{v}^{t}-\nabla f(x^{t}))+(1-\hat{\beta})\overline{g}^{t-1}+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}[\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})-(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})]\right\|

\displaystyle\overset{(ii)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t})\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\clip_{\tau}(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})-(v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1})\|

\displaystyle\overset{(iii)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t-1})\|+\hat{\beta}\|\nabla f(x^{t})-\nabla f(x^{t-1})\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|(1-\beta)v_{i}^{t-1}+\beta\nabla f_{i}(x^{t},\xi^{t}_{i})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\max\left\{\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\tau,0\right\},

(41)

where
(i)
follows from the update rule of
g_{i}^{t}
,
(ii)
– from the triangle inequality,
(iii)
– from the update rule of
v_{i}^{t},
triangle inequality, and
Lemma

1
. We continue the derivation of the bound as follows

\displaystyle\|\overline{g}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(iv)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2L(f(x^{t-1})-f^{\star})}+\hat{\beta}L\gamma\|g^{t-1}\|+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\left((1-\beta)\|v_{i}^{t-1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})\|+(1-\beta)\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t})\|+\beta\|\theta^{t}_{i}\|\right),

\displaystyle\overset{(v)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\hat{\beta}L\gamma(2-\beta)\|g^{t-1}\|+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\hat{\beta}\beta b

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}(1-\beta)\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t-1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})\|

\displaystyle\overset{(vi)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\hat{\beta}L\gamma(2-\beta)\|\overline{m}^{t-1}-g^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}L\gamma(2-\beta)\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|+(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)

(42)

where

follows from
L
-smoothness, the update rule of
x^{t}
, assumption 7 of the lemma, and triangle inequality,
(v)
– from assumptions 10 and 11 of the lemma,
L
-smoothness, the update rule of
x^{t}
,
(vi)
– from triangle inequality. Using (

) we continue

\displaystyle\|\overline{g}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(vii)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\sqrt{4L\Delta}+2L\gamma\hat{\beta}\left(2\hat{\beta}\tau T\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+2\hat{\beta}\sqrt{\frac{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t-1}\|^{2}}\right)+2L\gamma\hat{\beta}\|\overline{m}^{t-1}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\|\overline{g}^{t-1}\|+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\hat{\beta}\beta b+\frac{\hat{\beta}}{G}(1-\beta)\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|v_{i}^{t-1}-\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})\|,

(43)

where

follows from (
18
) and inequality
\sqrt{a+b}\leq\sqrt{a}+\sqrt{b}
for any positive
a,b
. Then, we get

\displaystyle\|\overline{g}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\overset{(viii)}{\leq}\hat{\beta}\sqrt{4L\Delta}+4L\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\tau T\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+4L\gamma\hat{\beta}^{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\hat{\beta}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\hat{\beta}\beta b

\displaystyle\qquad+\;4L\gamma\hat{\beta}\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\hat{\beta}a+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(1-\hat{\beta})\left(\sqrt{64L\Delta}+3(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+3b+3\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)\left(\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right),

where

\displaystyle\qquad+\;(B_{\rm init}-\tau)[\hat{\beta}+12L\gamma\hat{\beta}+3(1-\hat{\beta})+\nicefrac{{3\hat{\beta}(1-\beta)}}{{2}}]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}[4L\gamma\hat{\beta}+4L\gamma\hat{\beta}+4\hat{\beta}(1-\hat{\beta})]

For the second term, we have

(46)

where the last inequality holds by the choice of the step-size
24L\gamma\leq 1
.
For the third term, we have

Lemma 8
.

f_{i}
be

-smooth,

\|\nabla f_{i}(x^{t-1})-v_{i}^{t-1}\|\leq\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T

and

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|\leq\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\frac{\tau\hat{\beta}}{2}.

Proof.

Since
i\in\mathcal{I}_{t},
then
\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|>\tau
and from

4
we have

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;2\hat{\beta}\beta L\gamma\tau T\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}+2\beta\hat{\beta}L\gamma\sqrt{\frac{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}{G}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{G}}\|\Omega_{i}^{t}\|^{2}}+\beta\|\theta^{t+1}_{i}\|.

5
and
6
, we obtain from (

)

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\beta\left(\sqrt{4L\Delta}+\frac{3}{2}(B_{\rm init}-\tau)+\frac{3}{2}b+\hat{\beta}a+4\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}+\frac{3}{2}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{\beta}\tau T\right)

Regrouping the terms, we derive

\displaystyle\|v_{i}^{t+1}-g_{i}^{t}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\hat{\beta}\tau+\sqrt{L\Delta}[8L\gamma\beta+2\beta]+(B_{\rm init}-\tau)[3L\gamma\beta+\nicefrac{{3\beta}}{{2}}]

where we use

\displaystyle\qquad+\;b[3L\gamma\beta+\nicefrac{{5\beta}}{{2}}]+\hat{\beta}a[3L\gamma\beta+\beta]+\hat{\beta}\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}\hat{a}[3\beta L\gamma+4\beta]

\displaystyle\qquad+\;\hat{\beta}\tau T\sqrt{2c\delta_{\rm byz}}[5\beta L\gamma+\nicefrac{{3\beta}}{{2}}]

\displaystyle\leq\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\hat{\beta}\tau+4\cdot\frac{\tau\hat{\beta}}{12}+\frac{\tau\hat{\beta}}{6}

\displaystyle=\|v_{i}^{t}-g_{i}^{t-1}\|-\frac{\hat{\beta}\tau}{2}.

(62)
